[2] Global attention is shifting to the sub-continent for a variety of reasons, ranging from fast track economic growth, growing population and markets, the untapped socio-economic potential of the region and the growing energy consumption levels

[3] Alongside, some countries in the region have come to be the fountainhead of terrorism, thus inviting close attention of the world

[4] The interests and presence of major powers in this region, coupled with the relative instability in our immediate neighbourhood are a matter of concern to India

[5] India’s rapidly growing economy, relative size and strategic location in the region thus brings the country into prominence in the world’s geopolitics

[6] India, nevertheless, remains committed to peace and stability in the region, which are fundamental for the continued economic development and prosperity of its people

[7] The preceding year witnessed a series of developments which impinge directly on India’s security environment

[8] These include the deteriorating situation in Pakistan and the continued unrest in Afghanistan and Sri Lanka

[9] Stability and peace in West Asia and the Gulf, which host several million people of Indian origin and which is the primary source of India’s energy supplies, is of continuing importance to India

[10] The continuing threat posed by global terrorism underlines the importance of the need to devise cooperative efforts at a global level, between and among states

[11] There has been a marked deterioration in the internal situation of Pakistan, which has serious implications on overall stability and peace in the region

[12] The assassination of former Prime Minister Ms. Benazir Bhutto was a setback to the forces of political moderation

[13] The growth of extremist violence and terrorism in Pakistan has serious security implications for India

[14] India wishes to create an atmosphere of trust and confidence, free from violence and terror, in our bilateral relations with Pakistan and is working on various confidence building measures with our close neighbour

[15] As the world continues to shrink and get more and more interdependent due to globalisation and advent of modern day technologies, peace and development remain the central agenda for India

[16] India remains committed to peace and stability in the region, which are fundamental for the continued economic development and prosperity of its people

[17] China is an important player in global affairs and is proceeding ahead with her well charted out goals in a focused manner

[18] China has been improving bilateral relations with her neighbouring countries at diplomatic, economic and military levels

[19] In attempting to resolve the long outstanding border disputes, we are following a policy of constructive engagement

[20] Nepal is a natural ally of India and we share many common values and beliefs and also share an open border with Nepal

[21] The political situation in Nepal has undergone a major change in recent years and it is hoped that the situation should stabilise soon as instability in the region often has a spill over factor into our country

[22] Further, many citizens of Nepal serve in the Indian Army and have strong traditional people-to-people ties with Nepal

[23] India will provide assistance within her means, required by Nepal to rehabilitate the social, economic and political fabric of Nepal

[24] We have recently seen an increase in violence, tension and conflict in Sri Lanka

[25] We strongly believe that there is no military solution

[26] What is required is a settlement of the political, constitutional and other issues within the framework of a united Sri Lanka which addresses the concerns of all communities, especially the ethnic minority

[27] The security situation in Afghanistan has deteriorated in the recent past with the continued resurgence of the Taliban along the Pakistan-Afghanistan border

[28] Terrorist attacks mounted by the Taliban have consequently increased significantly all over Afghanistan

[29] We continue to support the process to help Afghanistan emerge as a stable democratic state

[30] India has a vital stake in peace and stability in this region and has invested the services of nearly 4000 Indians and US$ 750 million for the purpose of rebuilding Afghanistan

[31] Inimical interests are, however, attempting to scuttle our role towards rebuilding Afghanistan

[32] As a close and friendly neighbour of Bangladesh, we would like to see a peaceful, stable and a liberal democratic Bangladesh

[33] We hope the roadmap announced by the Bangladesh Election Commission with regard to the next parliamentary elections to be held between October and December 2008, leads to early restoration of democracy and relative stability in Bangladesh

[34] India is concerned about Bangladesh as she shares a boundary with many of our North-Eastern States

[35] Myanmar is of strategic significance to India

[36] We need to ensure a peaceful periphery for India, especially on the security front, since we share a 1640 km

[37] long border with her

[38] The political reform and national reconciliation process initiated by the Myanmar government should be taken forward expeditiously in a broad-based and inclusive manner including Ms. Aung San Suu Kyi and various ethnic groups

[39] Amongst our other neighbours are two other countries viz

[40] Bhutan and Maldives, who are continuing on the path to peace, development and stability

[41] India is providing economic and technical assistance to these countries in various fields

[42] India’s relations with these countries are based on a strong foundation of mutual understanding, respect and trust

[43] Apart from a conventional war scenario for which the armed forces are prepared, the increasing ongoing internal security situation of a proxy war scenario also has to be taken into account

[44] The security environment and instability in our neighbourhood could fuel disturbances for our internal security as well

[45] The growing menace of terrorism continues to be an area of great concern, where we are facing newer challenges

[46] Our armed forces are geared up to the challenges to meet this asymmetric threat

[47] The succeeding paragraphs describe the factors that have a bearing on our internal security

[48] The current situation in J&K is well under control

[49] The violence has been brought down considerably by effective counter infiltration operations along the Line of Control and sustained counter terrorist operations by the security forces in the hinterland

[50] This is a highly commendable achievement

[51] But despite assurances, terrorist activities in Jammu and Kashmir and other parts of the country continued to be sponsored and supported from across the country

[52] There has been marked improvement in the security situation in some States of the North Eastern region

[53] However, continued violence by some insurgent groups and their reported cross-border links in some States, such as Assam and Manipur is a matter of concern

[54] The armed forces therefore, continue to be deployed in aid to civil authorities in some area of the region

[55] While the Government is open to talks within framework of the Constitution with any groups which abjure violence, all efforts are being made to bring about further improvement in overall security environment through holistic approach

[56] The extremist activities of ‘Maoist/Naxalites’ have the potential for causing internal security problems in some areas of certain states

[57] In this context, funding through illegal flows and possible links involving in arms and smuggling are also matters of concern

[58] The Government is following a holistic security-cum-development oriented approach to deal with the problem

[59] The security situation in India’s extended neighbourhood of Mid-East and South-East Asia is being monitored as any deterioration in the region would adversely affect India’s economic and energy security

[60] Moreover, a large number of Indians serve in the countries of the Middle East and South East Asia - both regions with whom we share ancient civilisational ties

[61] The safety and stability of the region is not only a matter of our security and energy needs but also one of umbilical linkages with India

[62] The energy situation is in a state of instability

[63] We are expecting a steep rise in competition as the dependency increases along with the prices

[64] We also have a vital stake in the security of the sea-lanes to our East and West

[65] The Bay of Bengal is a region rich in natural gas and oil and could impact on the future energy calculations

[66] Today there are more challenges than opportunities to world peace and regional security

[67] As the world continues to shrink and get more and more interdependent due to globalisation and advent of modern day technologies, peace and development remain the central agenda for India

[68] With a steadily growing economy, India has a vital stake in a safe and secure world

[69] India and Indians have increasingly become drivers of global growth and prosperity

[70] We understand a strong defence force is a necessary prerequisite for growth, stability and peace

[71] India has been committed to maintain its level of defence preparedness to deter any type of threat both conventional as well as unconventional

[72] During the year we have also contributed several troops to the United Nations in support of peace across the world

[73] In the year gone by, India has made significant progress in expanding her defence cooperation with like-minded countries and has enhanced her ties with several countries across the world from within the Asia Pacific region to Europe, Americas and the African subcontinent not only to enhance the security environment of the region but also the global security scenario in order to maintain world peace, promote socio-economic collaboration and the overall development for the common good of all the people around the world

[74] ORGANISATION AND FUNCTIONS OF THE MINISTRY OF DEFENCE ORGANISATIONAL SET-UP AND FUNCTIONS 2.1 After independence, Ministry of Defence was created under the charge of a Cabinet Minister, and each Service was placed under its own Commander-in-Chief

[75] In 1955, the Commanders-in-Chief were renamed as the Chief of the Army Staff, the Chief of the Naval Staff and the Chief of the Air Staff

[76] In November 1962, a Department of Defence Production was set up to deal with research, development and production of defence equipment

[77] In November 1965, the Department of Defence Supplies was created for planning and execution of schemes for import substitution of defence requirements

[78] These two Departments were later merged to form the Department of Defence Production and Supplies

[79] In 2004, the name of Department of Defence Production and Supplies was changed to Department of Defence Production

[80] In 1980, the Department of Defence Research and Development was created

[81] In 2004, the Department of Ex-Servicemen Welfare was created

[82] The Defence Secretary functions as head of the Department of Defence and is additionally responsible for coordinating the activities of the four Departments in the Ministry

[83] The principal task of the Ministry is to frame policy directions on defence and security related matters and communicate them for implementation to the Services Headquarters, Inter-Service Organisations, Production Establishments and Research & Development Organisations

[84] It is required to ensure effective implementation of the Government’s policy directions and the execution of approved programmes within the allocated resources

[85] Ministry of Defence is required to ensure effective implementation of the Government’s policy directions on defence and security related matters and execution of approved programmes

[86] The principal functions of the Departments are as follows: (i

[87] The Department of Defence deals with the Integrated Defence Staff (IDS) and three Services and various Inter-Service Organisations

[88] It is also responsible for the Defence Budget, establishment matters, defence policy, matters relating to Parliament, defence cooperation with foreign countries and coordination of all defence related activities

[89] The Department of Defence Production is headed by a Secretary and deals with matters pertaining to defence production, indigenization of imported stores, equipment and spares, planning and control of departmental production units of the Ordnance Factory Board and Defence Public Sector Undertakings (DPSUs

[90] iii) The Department of Defence Research and Development is headed by a Secretary, who is the Scientific Adviser to the Raksha Mantri

[91] Its function is to advise the Government on scientific aspects of military equipment and logistics and the formulation of research, design and development plans for equipment required by the Services

[92] iv) The Department of Ex-Servicemen Welfare deals with all resettlement, welfare and pensionary matters of Ex-Servicemen

[93] A list of subjects dealt with by various departments and Finance Division of the Ministry of Defence is given in Appendix-I to this report

[94] Integrated Defence Staff (IDS) was created on October 1, 2001 as a sequel to the decision by the Group of Ministers based on Kargil Committee Report

[95] The staff of Headquarters IDS is provided from three Services, MEA, DRDO, Armed Forces Headquarters (AFHQ) Civil Services and DoD. IDS is presently functioning as staff in the advisory mode to the Chairman COSC, and is headed by Chief of Integrated Defence Staff to Chairman COSC (CISC

[96] Jointness within the Services: One of the primary objectives of HQ IDS is to promote jointness within the Services

[97] Jointness is aimed at obtaining optimal operational and cost effectiveness in the current and envisaged future geopolitical environment

[98] It will also enable the services to operate seamlessly with common understanding of missions and joint strategies to accomplish them

[99] This would entail bringing about transformation and reforms within the armed forces

[100] The vertical and horizontal aspects of integration have been identified and are being put into place in an organized and logical manner through the establishment of inter-service committees, functioning in and with HQ IDS

[101] HQ IDS has achieved a number of milestones within a short span in promoting the concept of jointness within the services

[102] Defence Planning Process: The Defence Planning Process is being strengthened as part of the follow up to the Group of Minister’s Report on Security

[103] As a first step, Draft National Security Strategy has been prepared and forwarded to National Security Adviser

[104] Joint Doctrines: Joint Doctrine for Defence Forces and Joint Amphibious Warfare Doctrine have already been released whereas the Joint Special Forces Doctrine, Joint Psychological Operations Doctrine, Joint Subconventional Warfare Doctrine and Joint Space Doctrine are in advance stages of preparation

[105] Defence Space Vision (DSV) 2020: HQ IDS has been nominated as the tri Service single window for interaction in space by all agencies including external ones

[106] An Integrated Space Cell has been established to coordinate space issues and formulate a Draft Space Doctrine

[107] Long Term Integrated Perspective Plan (LTIPP): A LTIPP focusing on the Joint Conventional Edge, capabilities to be achieved, aspects of commonality of equipment; inter-Service prioritization and indigenization has been prepared to cover the 11th, 12th and 13th Plan periods

[108] Integration between DRDO and Services: HQ IDS has carried out analysis of DRDO’s 11th Plan and a sincere effort to synchronize it with the Services 11th Defence Plan has been made

[109] Joint Intelligence Assessments: With the establishment of the Defence Intelligence Agency, integration of service intelligence agencies and a common assessment of strategic intelligence inputs is now available to defence planners

[110] Joint Exercises: A number of joint exercises with other countries have been conducted by HQ IDS

[111] The latest, AMPHEX 07, was a major tri service exercise undertaken to validate the Joint Amphibious Doctrine

[112] Think Tank Organisation: Centre for Joint Warfare Studies (CENJOWS) was inaugurated on August

[113] The main objectives of CENJOWS, which will function under HQ IDS, are conduct of studies and research work in Joint War-fighting, organisation of orientation courses/capsules for various Ministries/Agencies connected with HQ IDS/Services and promotion of Jointmanship amongst Services, Ministries and Intelligence Agencies involved in National Security

[114] Promoting Jointness: To promote jointness at all levels within the Services HQ IDS has launched ‘The Purple Pages’, its bi-annual magazine on jointness

[115] The magazine aims at providing a forum for all members of the services and the strategic community to share their views on the existing jointness and also propose options for the way ahead

[116] The three Services Headquarters, viz., the Army Headquarters, the Naval Headquarters and the Air Headquarters function under the Chief of the Army Staff (COAS), the Chief of the Naval Staff (CNS) and the Chief of the Air Staff (CAS) respectively

[117] They are assisted by their Principal Staff Officers (PSOs

[118] The Inter-Service Organisations, under the Department of Defence are responsible for carrying out tasks related to common needs of the three Services such as medical care, public relations and personnel management of civilian staff in the Defence Headquarters

[119] A number of Committees dealing with defence related activities assist the Raksha Mantri

[120] The Chiefs of Staff Committee is a forum for the Service Chiefs to discuss matters having a bearing on the activities of the Services and also to advise the Ministry

[121] The position of Chairman of the Chiefs of Staff Committee devolves on the longest serving Chief of Staff, and consequently rotates amongst the three Services

[122] To facilitate the work of the Chiefs of Staff Committee, a number of sub-committees have been established

[123] Information regarding the Ministers in the Ministry of Defence, the Chiefs of Staff, the Secretaries in the Departments of the Ministry and the Secretary (Defence Finance)/Financial Advisor (Defence Services) who held positions from April 1, 2007 onwards is given in Appendix II to this report

[124] Finance Division in the Ministry of Defence deals with all matters having financial implications

[125] It is fully integrated with the Ministry of Defence and performs an advisory role

[126] To achieve greater efficiency in administration, expeditious disposal and transparency, Ministry of Defence exercises financial powers in consultation with the Finance Division as per the procedure laid down in the Defence Procurement Procedure 2006 and Defence Procurement Manual

[127] The Defence Procurement Procedure deals with capital acquisitions and Defence Procurement Manual deals with revenue procurements

[128] E-Procurements is one of the Thrust Areas approved by the Prime Minister for the year

[129] Accordingly, steps have been taken to enhance the scope of e-procurement in DRDO

[130] This would facilitate tender inquiries being sent electronically to a bigger than usual set of vendors and improve competition/transparency as well as cut down delays

[131] Pilot projects for procurement of common user items have been taken up in five administrative units and designated officers have been entrusted with digital signatures

[132] Finance Division prepares and monitors Defence Services Estimates, Civil Estimates of the Ministry of Defence and the Estimates in respect of Defence Pensions

[133] Break-up of the actual expenditure for the years 2005-06 and 2006-07, as also the Revised Estimates for 2007-08 and Budget Estimates for 2008-09 are given in the Table No. 2.1 and charts at the end of this chapter

[134] Summary of latest report of the Comptroller & Auditor General on the working of the Ministry of Defence is given in Appendix III to this Annual Report

[135] Establishment of Armed Forces Tribunal 2.26 Armed Forces Tribunal Bill, 2005 was introduced in the Rajya Sabha in September

[136] The Standing Committee of Parliament on Defence examined the Bill and gave its recommendations in May

[137] The Ministry considered the recommendations and moved certain amendments in the Rajya Sabha in December

[138] Both the Houses have passed the Bill in the Winter Session of the Parliament in December

[139] The Armed Forces Tribunal Act, 2007 has been notified on 28 December

[140] Necessary follow up action is being taken on priority to set up the Principal Bench of the Armed Forces Tribunal in New Delhi

[143] It is a voluntary service

[144] The army has rich combat experience in diverse terrains, due to India’s diverse geography and also has a distinguished history of serving in United Nations Peacekeeping Operations

[145] Through its large, sustained troop commitments, India has come in for much praise for taking part in difficult UN Peacekeeping Operations for prolonged periods

[146] The basic responsibility of the Army is to safeguard the territorial integrity of the nation against external aggression

[147] Due to country’s long borders encompassing different geographical and climatic conditions such as desert terrain on the west, snow-covered mountains in the north and thick rainfed mountainous jungles in the east, the Army has to constantly prepare itself for diverse challenges

[148] In addition, the Army is often required to assist the civil administration during internal security disturbances and in the maintenance of law and order, in organising relief operations during natural calamities like floods, earthquakes and cyclones and in the maintenance of essential services

[149] Demands on the Army have increased manifold due to continuous deployment of its forces in counterinsurgency operations in Jammu & Kashmir and the North East parts of the country

[150] To achieve these objectives, the Army has to be constantly modernised, suitably structured, equipped and trained

[151] MODERNISATION OF ARMY Indian Army is one of the finest Armies in the world

[152] Modernisation and upgradation of Army is a continuous process to keep the Armed Forces ready to meet any challenge of tomorrow

[153] It is based on five year plans

[154] Focus and core areas of modernisation has been on:- (a) Improvement in Fire Power and increased Mobility

[155] b) All Weather Battle Field Surveillance capability. (c) Night Fighting capabilities

[156] d) Enhance capability of Special Forces

[157] e) Capability for Network Centric Warfare

[158] f) NBC Protection

[159] The Indian Army is well equipped with modern technologies/equipment to increase their combat capabilities

[160] Mechanised Forces: Image Intensification (II) and Thermal Imaging (TI) Night Vision Devices and weapon sights are essential to enhance the ability of our Mechanised Forces to operate by night

[161] Procurement of a range of night vision equipment for the T-72 and T-55 tanks as well as the integration of existing TI sight with other weapon system for Infantry Combat Vehicle (ICV) BMP-2 is being carried out on priority

[162] Super Generation II Sight for tanks is also being procured

[163] The mobility and navigational capabilities of T-72 tanks and ICV BMP-2 are being enhanced by upgradation of their Power Pack, GPS and Advanced Land Navigation Systems

[164] To enhance efficiency and to make training more cost effective, BMP Driving Simulator has been contracted and scheme for Gunnery Simulators is at advanced stage of procurement

[165] Artillery: Acquisition of additional Unmanned Aerial Vehicles (UAVs) and Night Vision Surveillance Devices by Artillery will enhance the Surveillance and Target Acquisition capability

[166] The main focus for the Artillery is to acquire heavy calibre Guns with enhanced ranges with better firepower mobility

[167] Army Air Defence (a) Air Target Imitator (ATI) Launcher: ATI Launcher of Russian origin based on BM-13 vehicle was issued along with the Kvadrat equipment. (b) Modification for carriage of SAM-7 Missiles by Civil Hired Kvadrat Missile Firing Transport (CHT

[168] During mobilisation, SAM-6 missiles are required to be carried by 9 tonne CHTs

[169] A modification kit has been fabricated with help of which, three SAM-6 missiles can be carried by a 9 tonne CHT/ALS vehicle

[170] Army Aviation: The capabilities of Army Aviation are being strengthened by replacing existing Utility Helicopters with higher capacity Helicopters and induction of Armed Helicopter for special operations as well as Tactical Battle Support Operations. 3.8 Engineers: Army is on the way to increase its mine-laying and breaching capabilities

[171] Procurement of State-of-the-art Influence Mines has enhanced the stopping power of our minefields

[172] Acquisition of various bridging expedients will enhance tactical mobility of Strike Formations in the battle

[173] State-of-the-art Counter Improvised Explosive Device (IED) equipment has been inducted

[174] Signals: The Corps of Signals has taken a number of major strides in fielding the Information and Communication Technology (ICT) infrastructure and facilities that will enable transformation of Indian Army into a Network Enabled Force by

[175] The communication infrastructure has been enhanced by completion of two major projects

[176] An Optical Fibre Cable (OFC) communication network has been established in strategic alliance with BSNL in the harsh terrain of Eastern sector

[177] Another major achievement has been establishment of Army owned Mobile Cellular Communication System in Northern Sector

[178] The system provides 24 x 7 voice connectivity to the brave soldiers operating in mountainous terrain

[179] Infantry: The combat potential of Infantry and Rashtriya Rifles is being comprehensively transformed through a quantum enhancement of surveillance, firepower, protection, communication and mobility requirements

[180] In pursuit for modernisation, the Infantry Battalions are being provided state-of-the-art weapon systems of greater lethality, range and precision, thermal imaging devices, bullet and mine proof vehicles and secure radio communications

[181] The Territorial Army (TA) is a voluntary, part time Army consisting of otherwise gainfully employed Indian citizens, eager to perform their role in the country’s defence in the event of a national emergency by relieving the Regular Army of their static duties and to aid the civil authorities in dealing with natural calamities and maintenance of essential services

[182] Home and Hearth Battalions (TA): A few Home and Hearth Battalions have been raised in addition to one Ikhwan Battalion. 3.13 Ecological Task Forces: Two ecological task force units have been raised for Assam

[183] These units are being funded by the Ministry of Environment and Forests

[184] The Ecological Task Force is making significant contribution in checking the ecological degradation in the assigned project areas

[185] The forestation task is undertaken by the ex-servicemen recruited in these units thus providing them an employment avenue

[186] RASHTRIYA RIFLES (RR) 3.14 Thaw in relations with our adversaries and continued ceasefire on LoC has given a new dimension to the role of RR in J&K. Rashtriya Rifles has surpassed its past performance in countering the terrorist threat in the hinterland

[187] RR's contribution in winning of hearts and minds has also been well recognised and applauded

[188] Rashtriya Rifles remains the Counter Insurgency/ Counter Terrorism Arm of the Indian Army

[189] Operational Performance: Operational performance of Rashtriya Rifles has been exemplary

[190] This has been possible due to high motivational level of troops, a good intelligence network and an excellent rapport with local population and civil administration

[191] A number of tours to include elders, women and children were organized to give an exposure to the insulated populace

[192] Health care of the locals has been a major concern of Rashtriya Rifles

[193] Towards this end, at every Battalion Headquarters, the Regiment Medical Officer renders medical assistance and aid to villagers in their respective areas of responsibility

[194] In addition, several medical-cum-veterinary camps have been conducted

[195] The physically challenged persons have been gifted with wheelchairs, tricycles and some fitted with artificial limbs, in concert with the Artificial Limb Manufacturing Corporation, Kanpur

[196] COUNTER INSURGENCY OPERATIONS AND INTERNAL SECURITY SITUATIONS

[197] The contours of our security challenges are numerous and varied

[198] To the military complexities arising out of the problems of unsettled borders, the challenges of the proxy war in Jammu & Kashmir, the insurgency in the North East and the growing Naxal menace in Central India, the ever enlarging spectre of terrorism and numerous non-military threats to our security have also been added

[199] Green Siachen – Clean Siachen" Major plantation drive in the barren reaches of Nubra and Shyok valleys Jammu & Kashmir: The ceasefire on the borders is holding out, with a few minor aberrations

[200] Our resolve in upholding the ceasefire has not only helped to strengthen the ongoing peace initiative but has also given people along the Line of Control, the opportunity to re-establish their lives

[201] Intelligence assessments indicate continued existence of terrorist infrastructure and camps across the borders

[202] Pakistan retains the ability to calibrate the intensity of the proxy war

[203] A high level of vigil is being maintained on the borders so that infiltration from Pakistan Occupied Kashmir (POK) is minimized

[204] The security forces have been able to significantly curtail the freedom of action of terrorists in J&K. Violence levels, as a consequence, have reduced significantly

[205] The strength of the terrorists as well as their potential, in terms of weapons and related infrastructure, has shown a perceptible decline

[206] Our strategy, which essentially emphasises the need for ‘people centricity’ in operations, is paying rich dividends

[207] Our focus continues to be on the conduct of surgical operations based on hard intelligence while causing minimum inconvenience to the local populace

[208] The Indian Army is committed to avoidance of public alienation while respecting human rights scrupulously

[209] Tourist traffic has been encouraging and the economy is showing definite signs of picking up

[210] Revival of local bodies, public outrage against terrorist attacks on tourists, protests seeking better administration and governance, resistance by families against recruitment of terrorists, are positive indicators of normalcy returning to the State

[211] The upswing in tourism and the successful management of the Amarnath Yatra (over three lakh devotees this year) are other visible indicators of normalcy

[212] The local population is also coming forward to provide real time intelligence on terrorist activities, which in turn has led to the launch of successful operations and elimination of top terrorist leaders

[213] To the military complexities arising out of the problems of unsettled borders, the challenges of the proxy war in Jammu & Kashmir, the insurgency in the North East and the growing Naxal menace in Central India, the ever enlarging spectre of terrorism and numerous non-military threats to our security have also been added

[214] North-East: The multidimensional approach adopted by the Government to address the insurgency problems in the North Eastern States has brought about a perceptible change in the overall security environment in the region

[215] There has been a marked reduction in violence levels and the general situation in the North-East States is well under control

[216] The people are increasingly expressing their desire for peace while resisting violence and extortions by the armed groups

[217] Assam: In Assam, operations by the Security Forces since the beginning of this year have dealt a severe blow to the ULFA

[218] Major successes have been recorded in ULFA strongholds in Upper Assam and the Lohit district of Assam

[219] In Nagaland, the peace talks continue and the ceasefire with NSCN (IM) has been extended indefinitely while that with NSCN (K) has been extended upto April

[220] Most of the violence in the State is on account of clashes between various factions of Under Ground (UG) groups

[221] Efforts are on to confine the cadres to their camps and strengthen the Ceasefire Monitoring Mechanisms

[222] Manipur: In Manipur, operations have been carried out in the Samtal Salient to clear the area of Under Ground group influence

[223] Concurrently, efforts are on to formalise Ceasefire Ground Rules with SoO groups under the aegis of the State Government and the Ministry of Home Affairs

[224] Security Forces have played a major role in creating sustained operational pressure on most of the terrorist groups in the North Eastern States

[225] This has shaped the environment for effecting surrenders by the terrorists

[226] Naxal Violence

[227] In so far as Naxal Violence is concerned, the Indian Army has been instrumental in strengthening police mechanisms across the States

[228] We have extended advice and training in Counter Naxal Operations, Counter IED Operations, Training of Trainers, as also CRPF, PAC and IRP battalions

[229] Companies have already been trained while an additional 65 Companies will be trained by June

[230] SITUATION ALONG THE LINE OF ACTUAL CONTROL (LAC

[231] The situation along the LAC remains peaceful

[232] In an attempt to forge better ties with China, a policy of positive engagement is being followed

[233] The institutionalisation of Confidence Building Measures and the signing of an ‘Agreement on Political Parameters and Guiding Principles for the Settlement of the India - China Boundary Question’, have given added impetus to the process

[234] Relations between the two nations have improved greatly over the past few years

[235] The Annual Defence Dialogue seeks to take the process of military-to-military interaction even further

[236] Consequent to the signing of ‘MoU on Exchanges and Cooperation in the Field of Defence’ signed during the visit of Hon'ble RM to China in May 2006, and the discussion during the visit of the COAS in May 2007, a need was felt by both the countries to finalise the interaction between the two Armed Forces

[237] The first India-China annual Defence Dialogue was held on November 12, 2007 at Beijing, China

[238] Annual Defence Dialogue for the year 2008 has been planned to be held in India

[239] The major issues discussed during the meeting focused on enhancing interaction between the Armed Forces, Annual Programme of exchange of visits, joint military training/exercise, participation in training courses, Sports & Adventure activities

[240] We continue to realistically analyse the growing economic and military capacities of China and the infrastructural developments in Tibet

[241] Accordingly, we are constantly reviewing and upgrading our strategic and conventional postures so that our national security is not compromised

[242] We are committed to consolidation of our manpower and force structures so as to rightsize our force and give it the necessary punch in consonance with the dictates of the modern battlefield

[243] The induction of long range artillery/rocket and missile weapon systems with their precision capacities is a step in that direction

[398] INDIAN AIR FORCE SU-30 MKI in flight

[399] The past 75 years have been an eventful journey for the Indian Air Force (IAF) – from a flight of ‘Wapitis’ in 1932, to the fourth largest, professionally acclaimed, strategic Air Force responsible for guarding Nation’s vital interests

[400] From 1948 to Kargil, the IAF has always fielded winning capabilities

[401] IAF’s professional and prompt operations in peace time, at home and abroad and in peacekeeping, have earned many accolades

[402] In recent years, IAF has enhanced cooperation with Air Forces of other countries and exercised with some of the best, to hone skills, assimilate ‘best practices’ and strengthen ties of friendship all over the World

[403] Today, the IAF has formal cooperation with six nations and exchanges with many others

[404] There are IAF teams and missions in 10 countries and four widely appreciated UN missions in Congo and Sudan

[405] To remain competitive and credible, it is imperative that the IAF imbibe modern technologies to achieve long-reach, precision, networked and space-enabled force capabilities

[406] The changing global environment, Regional military capabilities and vital national interests, necessitate a transformational modernization

[427] FLIGHT SAFETY** 5.15 Accident Statistics: The accident rate of the IAF has shown a steady decline from 1.48 per 10,000 hours in 1971-72 to 0.34 in

[428] The rate has further gone down to 0.31 per 10,000 hours in the current financial year (from April 1, 2007 to November

[429] This feat has been achieved by instituting various proactive measures by the IAF to avoid/minimize accidents, combined with a whole hearted and synergic effort by its personnel

[430] Anti Bird Measures: Various bird control measures adopted in the IAF have shown encouraging results

[431] Detailed survey of birds at flying stations by professionals, both in-house as well as external (like Bombay Natural History Society etc.) have resulted in bringing down bird hit accidents from three in 1971-72 to nil in

[432] The accident rate of the IAF has shown a steady decline from 1.48 per 10,000 hours in 1971-72 to 0.34 in

[433] Vermiculture: In order to scientifically dispose off the garbage at the Air Force Stations and thus manage bird activity, IAF launched a Zero-Garbage Scheme for its bases

[434] A team formed by DG (I&S) has suggested modalities for its implementation

[435] Under this scheme, the garbage is segregated at source into bio-degradable and non-degradable garbage

[436] The biodegradable garbage is then taken to the Vermiculture site, where it is converted into eco-friendly vermin compost with the help of worms

[437] The non-degradable garbage is disposed off at a place determined by the local municipality

[438] This scheme has been implemented at all major Air Force Stations and it has made the environment cleaner and reduced the bird activity

[439] Solid Waste Management: Effective management of solid waste at IAF flying stations as a part of anti-bird measures is under progress

[440] In 2001, IAF initiated a bird survey

[441] The Solid Waste Management Scheme was conceptualized and was envisaged to be completed in two years (2003-2004) with 100% central assistance

[442] An amount of Rs 99.35 crores had been earmarked towards the same

[443] At present the project has been completed and commissioned and functioning successfully at two airbases, i.e., Sirsa and Jodhpur

[444] Conduct of IFSCON: International Flight Safety Conference-2007 (IFSCON-07) was conducted in October, 2007 with professionals from 31 countries as well as Indian stake holders attending it

[445] The conference gave an opportunity to interact with aviation experts from countries across the globe with an aim to derive maximum benefit for the cause of aviation safety in the IAF. IFSCON-07, apart from giving significant impetus to aviation safety in India, also contributed to fostering mutually beneficial cooperation amongst the participating nations and enhanced the country’s international image

[495] COAST GUARD ‘IN STEP’- Coast Guard Republic Day Contingent The Indian Coast Guard has a force level of 43 ships, 45 aircraft and helicopters, 18 boats/craft and 23 non-commissioned boats/craft in its fleet

[496] The Indian Coast Guard came into being on February 1, 1977 on the approval of Cabinet Committee on Parliamentary Affairs to set up an interim Coast Guard Organisation under Naval Headquarters with an Officer on Special Duty of the rank of Vice Admiral as the head, pending the approval of the Plan for Coast Guard Organisation

[497] The Coast Guard was commissioned as an independent service on August 19, 1978 under the Coast Guard Act

[498] Since its inception, the Coast Guard has acquired a wide range of capabilities both surface and airborne to undertake the assigned tasks during peace time and to supplement the efforts of Indian Navy during war

[499] The command and control of the Coast Guard lies with the Director General of Indian Coast Guard at New Delhi

[500] The Organisation has three Regional Headquarters i.e., Mumbai, Chennai and Port Blair

[501] The three Regional Headquarters exercise command and control in the waters adjoining the entire coastline of India, through 11 Coast Guard Districts

[502] DUTIES AND FUNCTIONS

[503] The duties of Coast Guard are as follows: (a) Ensuring the safety and protection of artificial islands, offshore terminals, installations and other structures and devices in maritime zones

[504] b) Providing protection to fishermen including assistance to them at sea while in distress

[505] c) Taking such measures as are necessary to preserve and protect the maritime environment and to prevent and control marine pollution

[506] d) Assisting the customs and other authorities in anti-smuggling operations

[507] e) Enforcing the provisions of such enactments as are for the time being in force in the maritime zones

[508] f) Such other matters, including measures for the safety of life and property at sea and collection of scientific data, as may be prescribed

[509] EXISTING FORCE LEVEL

[510] The Indian Coast Guard has a force level of 43 ships, 45 aircraft and helicopters, 18 boats/craft and 23 non-commissioned boats/craft in its fleet to carry out regular surveillance of the Maritime Zones of India and the areas of interest

[511] One Advanced Offshore Patrol vessel 'Sankalp', constructed by M/s Goa Shipyard Limited (GSL), Goa is also being commissioned

[512] The other new incumbents to be brought into the Coast Guard force include - one Pollution Control Vessel – ‘Samudra Prahari’ and one Advanced Offshore Patrol Vessel – ‘Samrat’, launched on March 21, 2007 and July 2, 2007 respectively

[513] OPERATIONS AND EXERCISES

[514] The Indian Coast Guard carried out the following exercises during the year 2007: (a) Sagar Manthan IV: A national level pollution response exercise was conducted from April 9 to 10, 2007 off Sikka, Gulf of Kutch

[515] b) Operation Prakshepan: Indian Coast Guard Ship Habbah Khatun was tasked off Chennai to sanitise the danger area of fishing and shipping traffic view of launch of Polar Satellite Launch Vehicle C-8 from SDSC SHAR on April 23, 2007. (c) Search & Rescue Exercise (SAREX-07): National level Maritime Search and Rescue Exercise was conducted off Port Blair on November 15 and 16, 2007 to assess the capabilities of Indian Coast Guard to respond to SAR incident at sea

[516] National Maritime Search and Rescue (NMSAR) board members actively participated in the exercise and resources from other NMSAR agencies were also utilized to ensure a synergized response to the simulated situation

[517] d) Joint Exercise with OMAN

[518] Indian Coast Guard Ship Varuna, with integral helicopter and Savitribai Phule, departed Porbandar on April 14, 2007 for overseas deployment to Oman

[519] The ships visited Oman (Muscat) from April 16 to

[520] A joint exercise was conducted off Muscat between Indian Coast Guard and Royal Oman Police Coast Guard on April

[521] e) SAHAYOG Kai Jin-VIII: Indian Coast Guard Ship Sagar sailed from Port Blair on May 5, 2007 for overseas deployment to Vietnam, Japan and Philippines from May 11 to June 12, 2007 for Japan Coast Guard Sea Review and Combined Exercises. (f) IMDEX-07: Coast Guard Ship Sangram sailed from Port Blair on May 11, 2007 for overseas deployment to Singapore for participation in the International Maritime Defence Exhibition (IMDEX Asia-2007) at Singapore from May 14 to May

[522] Advanced Offshore Patrol vessel ‘Sankalp’, Pollution Control Vessel ‘Samudra Prahari’ and Advanced Offshore Patrol Vessel ‘Samrat’, are the new incumbents to be brought into the Coast Guard force

[523] g) ICGS Samar: Coast Guard Ship Samar sailed from Mumbai on August 10, 2007 for overseas deployment to Mauritius, Madagascar and Seychelles from August 21 to September 4, 2007. (h) ICGS Sangram: Coast Guard ship Sangram, with integral helicopter, sailed from Port Blair on November 25, 2007 for overseas deployment to Republic of Korea for joint exercise with Korea Coast Guard from December 10 to 14, 2007 to have interaction on Coast Guard-centric subjects with law enforcement agencies of Hong Kong and Singapore enroute

[536] Space Capsule Recovery Experiment: The Space Capsule Recovery Experiment (SRE) is a national project and a prelude to the Indian ‘Man on Moon’ mission – ‘Chandrayan’

[537] The SRE project was commenced by Indian Space Research Organisation (ISRO) in the year 2003 and the Indian Coast Guard has provided all assistance to ISRO since commencement of the project

[543] DEFENCE PRODUCTION Weapon Locating Radar manufactured by BEL The Department of Defence Production deals with the indigenization, development and production of defence equipment both in the public and private sectors. 7.1 The Department of Defence Production deals with the indigenization, development and production of defence equipment both in the public and private sectors

[544] The Department has 8 Defence Public Sector Undertakings and 39 ordnance factories with a wide-ranging production infrastructure

[545] The products include aircraft and helicopters, warships, submarines, heavy vehicles and earthmovers, missiles, a variety of electronic devices and components for the defence sector and alloys and special purpose steel

[546] Since Independence, the defence production sector has been developing steadily, with the objective of achieving self-reliance

[547] The following are the major organisations directly under the Department of Defence Production: - Ordnance Factory Board - Hindustan Aeronautics Limited - Bharat Electronics Limited - BEML Limited - Mazagon Dock Limited - Goa Shipyard Limited - Garden Reach Shipbuilders & Engineers Limited - Bharat Dynamics Limited - Mishra Dhatu Nigam Limited - Directorate General of Quality Assurance - Directorate General of Aeronautical Quality Assurance - Directorate of Standardisation - Defence Exhibition Organisation Defence equipment today is technology intensive with high levels of quality

[548] The Directorates General of Quality Assurance and Aeronautical Quality Assurance, and the Directorate of Standardisation have been set up to ensure these quality levels

[549] With the introduction of the new Defence Procurement Procedure 2006, Government has stipulated a 30% offset for contracts exceeding Rupees 300 crore

[550] The vendors concerned will have to source goods or services to this extent from Indian defence industry

[551] This would give the industry a significant opportunity to improve exports as well as manufacturing capabilities

[552] The Defence Exhibition Organisation has regularly been organizing two major international events “Defexpo” and “Aero India” since

[553] The Defexpo is a biennial event in which a large number of manufacturers, participating countries and businessmen take part; its focus is on land and naval systems

[554] Aero India, on the other hand, is for the aviation sector and aerospace

[555] Over the years, both events have secured considerable international recognition and have also grown significantly in terms of the number of participants and the area occupied

[556] ORDNANCE FACTORIES The Ordnance Factories Organization is the largest and oldest departmentally run production organization in the country

[557] It is primarily engaged in the manufacture of Defence hardware for the Armed forces

[558] The Ordnance Factories were established with a mandate to ensure self-reliance in manufacturing of Defence hardware

[559] The Ordnance Factories Organization is a fine blend of old and state-of-the-art factories

[560] The first Ordnance Factory was established in 1801 at Cossipore, near Kolkata

[561] There are 39 Ordnance Factories, geographically distributed all over the country at 24 different locations

[562] Ordnance factory, Nalanda and ordnance factory, Korwa are in project stage

[563] Organization Structure: The Ordnance Factory Board has a Chairman and 9 functional Members

[564] Out of these, five members head operating divisions and four Members are for Staff functions

[565] The operating divisions are based on the main products/or group of products

[566] The five operating divisions are: Ammunition and Explosives (A&E) Armoured Vehicles (AV) Materials and Components (M&C) Ordnance Equipment Group of Factories (OEF) Weapons, Vehicles and Equipment (WV&E

[567] The staff functions are: Personnel Finance Planning and Material Management Project and Engineering and Technical Services

[568] In addition, the Government has constituted a special Board, with representation from the Ministry of Defence, Army and Defence Research and Development Organization (DRDO) for providing appropriate input on resource planning, upgrading technology of products and efficient functioning of OFB

[569] Human Resources: Ordnance Factories have a large pool of qualified and experienced personnel

[570] Total strength of personnel in ordnance factories is 1,11,841 as on April

[573] ‘B’ officers and staff

[574] All the 39 Ordnance Factories have training institutes for training industrial employees and trade apprentices

[575] Product Profile: The product range of Ordnance Factories is as under: Weapon Items: Small Arms (Rifles, Pistols, Carbines, Machine Guns), Tank Guns, Anti-Tank Guns, Field Howitzers, Artillery Guns, Mortars, Air Defence Guns and Rocket Launchers

[576] Ammunition Items: Ammunitions for all the above weapon systems, Rockets, Missile Warheads, Mortar Bombs, Pyrotechnique (Smoke, Illuminating, Signal), Grenades and Bombs for Air Force, Naval ammunition, propellant and fuzes

[577] Armoured & Transport Vehicles: Tank T-72 ‘Ajeya’, Tank T-90 ‘Bhishma’, Infantry Combat Vehicles, Armoured Ambulance, Bulletproof and Mineproof Vehicles, Special Transport Vehicles and Variants

[578] Troop Comfort Items: Parachute for Army & Air Force, High Altitude and Combat Clothing, Tents of Various Types, Uniforms and Clothing Items, Floats for Light Assault Bridges

[579] Optoelectronics: Optical Instruments and Opto-Electronic Devices/Fire Control Instruments for Armoured Vehicles, Infantry and Artillery Systems

[580] Others: Special Aluminium alloys for aviation and space industry, Field Cables, Water Bowsers etc. RURM at Heavy Vehicles Factory, Avadi alongwith DRDO officials and Arjun MBT team

[791] DIRECTORATE GENERAL OF QUALITY ASSURANCE 7.60 Directorate General of Quality Assurance (DGQA) is an Inter-Service Organisation

[792] DGQA is responsible for Quality Assurance of all defence stores and equipment, both imported as well as indigenous for the Army, Navy (excluding Naval Armaments) and common user items for the Air Force procured from private sector, public sector undertakings and Ordnance Factories

[793] It has, therefore, a vital role to play in defence preparedness of the country

[794] Organisational Structure and Functions: DGQA Organisation is structured into ten Technical Directorates, each of which is responsible for a distinct range of equipment

[795] The Technical Directorates are structured in three tiers for functional purposes, comprising their respective Headquarters, Controllerates and Field Quality Assurance Establishments

[796] In addition, there are Proof Establishments in case of Armament Discipline for carrying out proof of weapons and ammunition

[797] The tasks performed by the three tiers are complementary and are integrated to achieve maximum efficiency

[798] The essential functions performed by the Organisation are as follows: Quality Assurance of Defence Stores and Equipment procured indigenously or ex-import

[799] b) Rendering assistance in productionising of the DRDO developed projects

[800] c) Render technical advice to Service Headquarters and promote Standardization

[801] d) Investigation of Defects and rendering advice on remedial measures

[802] e) Preparation, Updating and Issue of Drawings, Specifications, Technical Publications and Quality-Related Instructions

[803] f) Issue of DGQA Approvals/Assignment List and Cataloguing of Defence Stores

[804] g) Provide technical guidance in formulation of General Staff Qualitative Requirement (GSQR), association during Trial Evaluation, Development, etc., extension of shelf life and post-production services of Defence stores
